Effects and Coeffects in Call-By-Push-Value (Extended Version) Journal: PACMPL CCS: Theory of computation Type theory
Cassia Torczon, Emmanuel Suárez Acevedo, Shubh Agrawal, Joey Velez-Ginorio, Stephanie Weirich

Abstract
. Effect and coeffect tracking integrate many types of compile-time analysis, such as cost, liveness, or dataflow, directly into a language’s type system. In this paper, we investigate the addition of effect and coeffect tracking to the type system of call-by-push-value (CBPV), a computational model useful in compilation for its isolation of effects and for its ability to cleanly express both call-by-name and call-by-value computations. Our main result is effect-and-coeffect soundness , which asserts that the type system accurately bounds the effects that the program may trigger during execution and accurately tracks the demands that the program may make on its environment. This result holds for two different dynamic semantics: a generic one that can be adapted for different coeffects and one that is adapted for reasoning about resource usage. In particular, the second semantics discards the evaluation of unused values and pure computations while ensuring that effectful computations are always evaluated, even if their results are not required. Our results have been mechanized using the Coq proof assistant.



This paper is an extended version of
Torczon et al. 2024b
.

1.
Introduction

Computations interact with the world in which they run. Sometimes the
computation does something the world can observe, known as
an
effect

(
Lucassen and Gifford 1988
)
, and sometimes computations demand
something that the world must provide, known as a

coeffect

(
Petricek et al. 2014
;
Brunel et al. 2014
;
Orchard and Eades III 2022
)
.
For example, running a computation might take time (a clock ticking is an
effect) and might require resources (using input parameters is a coeffect).

Some programming languages track effects and coeffects
statically. Frank
(
Convent et al. 2020
)
,
Koka
(
Leijen 2014
)
, and the Verse functional logic
language
(
Verse development team 2023
)
do this for effects such as state, exceptions,
divergence, and failure; Linear Haskell
(
Bernardy et al. 2017
)
does this for a
resource management coeffect, while Agda and Idris 2
(
Brady 2021
)
do
this for a relevancy coeffect. The Effekt
language
(
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
)
both tracks effects statically and
uses a limited form of coeffect tracking to ensure that effect handlers are
well-scoped. Finally, the Granule language
(
Orchard et al. 2019
)
uses monads and
comonads graded by abstract structures to track various effects and
coeffects in a flexible and expressive system.

We would like to update the type systems of existing languages with effect and
coeffect tracking by annotating their existing type systems. However, in
contrast to systems that use monads and comonads to isolate effectful and
coeffectful code from the rest of the language, we need an approach that is
descriptive and that does not restrict programmers in how they structure their
code.

Because effectful computation depends on evaluation order, precisely tracking
effects works best in a language that makes its “ambient monad” explicit,
such as Moggi’s computational lambda calculus
(
Moggi 1989
)
and
fine-grained CBV
(
Levy et al. 2003
)
. These systems separate inert
“values” from executable “computations” and include “return” and
“let” constructs to sequence evaluation. This “ambient monad” is part of the
structure of the language itself; all computations are monadic.

Levy’s Call-By-Push-Value (CBPV)
(
Levy 2003b
)
is a calculus
that makes both the ambient computational monad
and comonad

explicit. As above, it separates values from computations and uses “return”
and “let” constructs to track how computations manipulate values. However,
CBPV also includes thunks, which temporarily suspend computations and treat
them as values, for the opposite purpose; as a result all computations are
also comonadic. In CBPV, then, we can annotate these existing structures
directly to track effects and coeffects, instead of adding new features to the language.

CBPV is a low-level language and is appropriate for use as a compiler
intermediate
representation
(
Garbuzov et al. 2018
;
Rizkallah et al. 2018
)
.
Its distinction between values and computations allows CBPV to work with
strict and nonstrict language features explicitly, enabling it to model both
call-by-value and call-by-name languages with the same facility. Adding
effects and coeffects to CBPV would enrich this intermediate representation to
support program optimizations; for example, to justify dead code elimination
for pure code whose coeffect annotations mark it as unused.

The ability of CBPV to model both CBV and CBN also lets us observe how
evaluation order changes the way a program alters and makes demands on the
world. Levy characterizes the difference between values and computations with
the slogan: “a value
is
, a computation
does
.”
(
Levy 2003b
)
Our
interpretation of this slogan is that only computations may contain effectful
subcomponents—values must be pure throughout. Conversely, coeffects describe
the demands a program makes on its inputs, which are always values in CBPV.

CBPV uses separate types for values and computations. Values have

positive
types (for which we use the metavariable
\mathit{A}
), while
computations have
negative
types (for which we use
\mathit{B}
). These two
forms are connected via an adjunction: the thunk type
\mathbf{U}\,\mathit{B}
suspends a
computation as an inert value, and the type of return
\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}
creates a
fine-grained structure similar to monadic bind that threads values through
computations. Due to the structure of the adjunction, the combination

\mathbf{U}\,(\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A})
forms a monad and the combination
\mathbf{F}\,(\mathbf{U}\,\mathit{B})
forms a comonad

(
Levy 2003a
)
.

The duality between values and computations gives CBPV its power,
and it is reflected in the structures we use
to statically track effects and coeffects. For
effects, we add effect information
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}
to the thunk type
\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}
,
recording the latent effect of suspended computations. Similarly, to track
coeffects, we add coeffect information
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
to the returner type

\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathit{A}
, describing the demands subsequent
computation is allowed to make on the returned value.
With this augmentation, we will show that the types
\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;(\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A})
and

\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;(\mathbf{U}\,\mathit{B})
can encode the graded monads and comonads associated with
effect and coeffect tracking.

Following this duality, this paper begins with two mirrored halves and then
combines them. The first part (Section
2
) extends CBPV with
effect tracking and shows how we can recover the graded monad by grading the
thunk type with latent effects. The second part (Section
3
)
extends CBPV with coeffect tracking and recovers a graded comonad by grading
the returner type with latent coeffects; we also discuss modifications to the
system for resource tracking with coeffects (Section
4
). Finally, we combine the two
systems and explore their interaction (Section
5
). This paper is best read in color:
effects
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}
appear in red and coeffects
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
in blue. Without these
colorful annotations, the type system and semantics are the standard rules of
CBPV.

Along the way, we prove the following results about our extensions.

•

We prove
effect soundness
for our effect-annotated extension of CBPV,
demonstrating that the type-and-effect system accurately bounds what happens at runtime.
To do so, we define an environment-based big-step operational semantics for
CBPV instrumented to precisely track effects during evaluation, and we use a
logical relation to prove our soundness theorem. (Section
2.3
)

•

We prove that the standard translations from call-by-value (CBV) and
call-by-name (CBN) lambda calculi to CBPV are
type-and-effect
preserving
. Starting with a well-typed CBV or (monadic) CBN program, we can produce a
well-typed CBPV program with the same effects as the source program.
(Section
2.4
)

•

We prove
coeffect soundness
for a coeffect-annotated extension
of CBPV, demonstrating that the type-and-coeffect system accurately tracks the demands
a program may make on its inputs. We do so using an
environment-based big-step operational semantics for CBPV, where
the environment has been instrumented to track coeffects during evaluation.
(Section
3.1
)

•

We observe that our generic coeffect-tracking operational semantics behavior has
strange implications when reasoning about resource usage.
Therefore, we adapt the rules of our
semantics so that it does not demand resources for discarded values, providing
a better model of how the program uses its inputs in this coeffect.
(Section
4
)

•

We prove that the standard translations from both CBN and CBV to CBPV
are
type-and-coeffect
preserving for this updated coeffect system. Starting with a well-typed CBN or
CBV program, we can produce a well-typed CBPV program with the same coeffects.
(Section
4.1
)

•

We combine the ‘tick’ effect and resource tracking coeffect together into the same
CBPV type system and prove combined versions of the
results from each:
type-and-effect-and-coeffect soundness

and
type-and-effect-and-coeffect preservation
of the standard translations
from CBV and CBN. We extend this system with a new rule that does not demand
resources for unused
computations
, when they are effect-free.
Finally, we prove that our discarding semantics produces the same result and has the
same effects as our general semantics, justifying the soundness of our resource
accounting semantics. (Section
5
)

We are not the first to extend CBPV with effect tracking and our type system
is most similar to
Kammar and Plotkin 2012
and
Forster et al. 2017
. However, all other definitions and results of this paper are novel. In
particular, we have found little work that explores the interaction between
CBPV and coeffects. Furthermore, while we are able use the standard
translations to interpret CBV and CBN in CBPV, designing the effect and
coeffect systems so that these translations “just work” is a contribution of
this paper. Our approach to effect-and-coeffect soundness also differs from
prior work—we employ a novel environment-based big-step semantics for CBPV
that leads to short and straightforward proofs.

For simplicity, the effect systems in this paper only track clock effects, and
the coeffect systems only count variable usages. As a result, we do not
explore more sophisticated interactions between other forms of effects and
coeffects, such as local and global state
(
Nanevski 2003
)
, or
between information flow and nondeterminism, or between usage analysis and
errors
(
Gaboardi et al. 2016
)
.

The results of this paper have been formalized in Coq and are
available online
1
1

1

https://github.com/plclub/cbpv-effects-coeffects/
and archived on
Zenodo
(
Torczon et al. 2024a
)
.

2.
Call-by-push-value (CBPV) and effect tracking

In this section, we extend the type system of CBPV with effect tracking. Our
modifications to the base system, which are limited to reasoning about
effect annotations
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}
, are marked in red.

CBPV syntactically separates terms into
values

\mathit{V}
​, inhabiting positive
types
\mathit{A}
, and
computations

\mathit{M}
, inhabiting negative types
\mathit{B}
,
as shown by the following grammar.
2
2

2

effects/CBPV/syntax.v:ValTy,CompTy,Val,Comp
​

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\textit{value types}&\mathit{A}&::=&\texttt{unit}\ |\ \mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}\ |\ \mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\times\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\ |\ \mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\textit{computation types}&\mathit{B}&::=&\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}\ |\ \mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}\ |\ \mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathop{\&}\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\\
\textit{values}&\mathit{V}&::=&\mathit{x}\ |\ \{\mathit{M}\}\ ()\ |\ |\ (\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\ |\ \mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{V}\ |\ \mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{V}\\
\textit{computations}&\mathit{M}&::=&\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{M}\ |\ \mathit{M}\,\mathit{V}\ |\ \mathit{V}!\ |\ \mathbf{let}\;(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}})=\mathit{V}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathit{N}\\
&&|&\langle\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle\ |\ \mathit{M}.1\ |\ \mathit{M}.2\ |\ \mathbf{return}\,\mathit{V}\ |\ \mathit{x}\leftarrow\mathit{M}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{N}\ |\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}\\
&&|&\mathit{V};\mathit{M}\ |\ \mathbf{case}\,\mathit{V}\,\mathbf{of}\,\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\to\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathsf{;}\,\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\to\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\end{array}

Values in CBPV mostly correspond to the values found in a call-by-value
typed functional language, such as unit and positive products and sums of values.
CBPV values also include suspended computations, called

thunks
and written
\{\mathit{M}\}
.
(Variables always represent values, so they are always declared with value
types in the context.)

Computations in CBPV include abstractions (
\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{M}
), applications
(
\mathit{M}\,\mathit{V}
), elimination (
forcing
) of thunks (
\mathit{V}!
),
unit elimination (
\mathit{V};\mathit{M}
),
positive product elimination (
\mathbf{let}\;(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}})=\mathit{V}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathit{N}
),
and
positive sum elimination (
\mathbf{case}\,\mathit{V}\,\mathbf{of}\,\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\to\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathsf{;}\,\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\to\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
).
In addition to positive products,
CBPV also includes negative products, of type
\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathop{\&}\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
. These are
introduced with a pair of computations
\langle\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle
and eliminated by
projecting either the first or second component,
i.e.

\mathit{M}.1
or

\mathit{M}.2
.

Values can be threaded through computations. The
\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{V}
form
injects a value into a trivial computation. In the
“letin” construct, written
\mathit{x}\leftarrow\mathit{M}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{N}
,
the first subcomputation must evaluate to the form
\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{V}
, and
the second computation can then reference
\mathit{V}
.
An advantage of CBPV is that this bind-like
method of threading values through computations makes it readily extensible with effectful
language features.
Levy 2003b
;
Levy 2006
;
Levy 2022
demonstrates how to add
nontermination, nondeterminism, errors, I/O, state, and control effects to CBPV.
In each case, Levy extends the language with new computations and modifies the
operational semantics to account for the new features.

For simplicity, we describe a single effect in this paper, the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}

computation. This effect advances a virtual clock in the operational
semantics, simulating the cost of the program.

2.1.
CBPV: Type-and-effect System

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}

(value effect typing)

\frac{\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\,\in\,\Gamma}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-var}{{{eff-var}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\{\mathit{M}\}:\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-thunk}{{{eff-thunk}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}():\mathbf{unit}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-unit}{{{eff-unit}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\quad\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}(\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\times\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-pair}{{{eff-pair}}}
}{},}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{V}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-inl}{{{eff-inl}}}
}{},}\quad\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{V}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-inr}{{{eff-inr}}}
}{},}

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}

(computation effect
typing)

\frac{\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-abs}{{{eff-abs}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}\quad\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\,\mathit{V}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-app}{{{eff-app}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}!\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-force}{{{eff-force}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{V}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}}\;\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-ret}{{{eff-ret}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}\quad\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{N}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{x}\leftarrow\mathit{M}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{N}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}\;\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-letin}{{{eff-letin}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\times\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathop{,}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{N}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathbf{let}\;(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}})=\mathit{V}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathit{N}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-split}{{{eff-split}}}
}{},}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\quad\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\langle\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathop{\&}\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-cpair}{{{eff-cpair}}}
}{},width=3in}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathop{\&}\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}.1\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-fst}{{{eff-fst}}}
}{},}\ \mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathop{\&}\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}.2\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-snd}{{{eff-snd}}}
}{},}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathbf{unit}\quad\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{N}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V};\mathit{N}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-sequence}{{{eff-sequence}}}

}{},width=4in}

\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{Tick}}}}\;\mathbf{F}\,\mathbf{unit}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-tick}{{{eff-tick}}}
}{},}\qquad\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\mathit{B}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eff-sub}{{{eff-sub}}}
}{},width=3in}

Figure 1
.
CBPV typing and effect tracking
The typing rules for CBPV augmented with effect tracking

Our type-and-effect system
3
3

3

effects/CBPV/typing.v:VWt,CWt
​
for CBPV is
shown in Figure
1
. Under some typing context
\Gamma
,
this system assigns a value type to values (
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}
) and both a
computation type and effect to computations (
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}
), where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}
is an upper bound on the effects that could occur during the evaluation
of
\mathit{M}
. The judgement for values does not need an effect annotation
because values are pure. In rule
eff-thunk
, the thunk type
\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}

records the latent effect of a suspended computation.

Following
Katsumata 2014
, our system models effects using an arbitrary

preordered monoid
. This gives us an identity element
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon}
,
an associative combining operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, and a preorder relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}
that
respects the operation. We also include a primitive effect
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{Tick}}
produced by the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}

computation. However, the only parts of the system that are specific to this effect are the rules
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}
, which is our
only
effectful computation. All other rules are presented
generically and are adaptable to other effects and effectful computations (e.g. a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{Read}}
effect
produced by a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{read}}
computation).

Concretely, we could use the natural number monoid with the usual ordering, 0 as the
identity element
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}
, and addition as the combining operation to have our type system perform a cost
analysis. Using 1 as our model of the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{Tick}}
effect, the system would statically bound the number
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}
s that are evaluated. For example, the type system would tell us that the computation

\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}},\mathit{y}\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}\,\mathbf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}\rangle
advances the clock at most twice. If the first component of
the pair is projected, the type system overapproximates the effect produced during execution.
Note that to track other behaviors with our type system, we need only change our preordered monoid
accordingly (e.g. we could track possible effects with the power set monoid ordered by set inclusion).

Rules
eff-ret
and
eff-letin
motivate the choice of a monoid structure.
Returning a value has no effect, so the
effect of
\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{V}
should always be
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon}
.
Rule
eff-letin
must combine
effects because
\mathit{x}\leftarrow\mathit{M}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{N}
is the only computation in our system
with two subcomputations,
both of which may be effectful. Finally, because return and letin
satisfy identity and associativity
properties as
the building blocks of the CBPV monad,
we need these same properties in our effect structure.

Rule
eff-sub
allows for imprecision in the type system. That is, an effect
annotation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}
on the type of a program indicates that the program
will have
at most

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}
as its effect; it may have less. If the
type system determines that the computation will complete within 5 ticks, it
is also sound, but less precise, for it to say that it will complete within 7
ticks. Choosing the discrete ordering (i.e. using equality for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}
) forces the type system to
track effects precisely. Note that to allow the discrete ordering, we do not
assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}
from the effect structure. In other words, the
type system does not need
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}
to be the least effect, only an identity element for the combining operation.

This imprecision allows more programs to type check. In a program with
branching, different branches may have different effects. For example, in
rule
eff-cpair
, only one side of a computational pair will ever be
evaluated. However, for soundness, both computations must be typed with the same effect
(which may be an overapproximation due to subeffecting).

Unlike in effect systems for the
\lambda
-calculus, the latent effects of
function bodies are not recorded in function types. Instead, they are
propagated to the conclusion of rule
eff-abs
. This makes sense because
abstractions are not values in CBPV. From an operational sense, they are
computations that pop the argument off the stack before continuing
(
Levy 2003b
)
.

2.2.
Instrumented Operational Semantics and Effect Soundness

We next define a big-step,
environment-based
operational semantics for CBPV.
Here, an environment,
4
4

4

effects/CBPV/semantics.v:env
​

\rho
, is a mapping from variables to
closed values
,
5
5

5

effects/CBPV/semantics.v:VClos
​

\mathit{W}
, and can be thought of as a sequence of delayed substitutions. Closed values
include closures,
i.e.
suspended computations paired with closing
environments, as well as unit, positive products and sums of
closed values.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\textit{environments}&\rho&::=&\emptyset\ |\ \rho\mathop{,}\mathit{x}\mapsto\mathit{W}\\
\textit{closed values}&\mathit{W}&::=&()\ |\ \mathbf{clo}(\rho,\{\mathit{M}\})\ |\ (\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\ |\ \mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{W}\ |\ \mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{W}\\
\end{array}

This semantics is new but straightforward. Past presentations of CBPV define its
operational behavior using small-step, big-step, or stack-based semantics, but
all the ones we have found use immediate substitution
(
Levy 2022
)
.
We choose an
environment-based big-step semantics for two reasons. First, the big-step
structure corresponds closely to the structure of the type system; there is
only one rule of the operational semantics for each rule of the type
system. Together with the use of environments, this semantics eliminates the
need for substitution lemmas, leading to a remarkably straightforward
soundness proof (Section
2.3
). Second, the environment
lets us track the demands that computations make on their inputs in our
coeffect soundness proof (Section
3.1
). For example,
with resource usage, we can include annotations in the environment
that count how many times
the program accesses each variable during computation, mirroring
the annotations in the context in the type system. A substitution-based
semantics does not support this instrumentation.

Appendix
A
shows the definition of the operational
semantics. This semantics consists of two relations. The first relation,
6
6

6

effects/CBPV/semantics.v:EvalVal
​
written
\rho\vdash\mathit{V}\ \Downarrow\ \mathit{W}
, uses the provided environment
\rho
to “evaluate” a
value
\mathit{V}
to a closed value
\mathit{W}
. This operation is essentially a
substitution operation in that it replaces each variable found in the value
with its definition in the environment.

The second relation,
7
7

7

effects/CBPV/semantics.v:EvalClos
​
written
\rho\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}
, shows how computations
evaluate to
closed terminal computations
,
8
8

8

effects/CBPV/semantics.v:CClos
​

\mathit{T}
. Closed terminals
are computations that cannot step any further, such as
returned (closed) values and suspended abstractions and pairs.
The effect annotation

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}
on this relation counts the number of ticks that occur during
evaluation of
\mathit{M}
.
While suspended abstractions and pairs resemble closures, they are not
first class. Instead, they provide a convenient notation describing the
propagation of the environment during evaluation.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\textit{closed terminals}&\mathit{T}&::=&\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{W}\ |\ \mathbf{clo}(\rho,\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{M})\ |\ \mathbf{clo}(\rho,\langle\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle)\\
\end{array}

The operational semantics of the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}
computation is trivial—it merely
produces a unit value and a single
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{Tick}}
effect. Other computations either
produce no effect (as in rule
eval-eff-comp-abs
) or
combine the effects of their subcomponents (as in
rule
eval-eff-comp-app-abs
). As in the type-and-effect system, the only
rule that is specific to the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{Tick}}
effect is the rule for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}
.
All other effects in these rules are parameterized over the input monoid.

While the type system allows for imprecision, the operational semantics
precisely tracks the effects of computation.

2.3.
Type and Effect Soundness

We state our effect
soundness theorem as follows: closed, well-typed computations of type
\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}

return closed values and produce effects that are bounded by the type system.

Theorem 2.1 (Effect soundness
9
9

9

effects/CBPV/soundness.v:soundness
​
).

If
\varnothing\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}
then
\emptyset\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{W}\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}
.

The reason that this theorem is limited to type
\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}
is
because we do not assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}
in the preordered monoid. As a
result, at other types, the soundness theorem is more complex. For a general
type
\mathit{B}
, we know that if
\mathit{M}
evaluates to some terminal
\mathit{T}

with effect
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}^{\prime}
, then there is some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}^{\prime\prime}
where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}^{\prime}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}^{\prime\prime}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}
. This extra
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}^{\prime\prime}
is the latent effect
from the case where
\mathit{T}
is a closure.

The proof is simple and based on the following
logical relation, consisting of three functions defined
mutually over the structure of types: closed
values
\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
, closed terminal computations
\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}
, and
computations tupled with environments
\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}
.

Definition 2.2 (CBPV with Effects: Logical Relation
10
10

10

effects/CBPV/semtyping.v:LRV
​
).

\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\ }l}\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}\rrbracket&=&\{\ \mathbf{clo}(\rho,\{\mathit{M}\})&|\ (\rho,\mathit{M})\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\ \}\\
\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathbf{unit}\rrbracket&=&\{\ ()\ \}\\
\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\times\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket&=&\{\ (\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{2}}}})&|\ \mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket\ \textit{and}\ \mathit{W_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\ \}\\
\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket&=&\{\ \mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{W}&|\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket\ \}\ \cup\{\ \mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{W}\ |\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\ \}\\
\\
\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}&=&\{\ \mathbf{return}\,\mathit{W}&|\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\ \textit{and}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}\;\equiv\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}\ \}\\
\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}&=&\{\ \mathbf{clo}(\rho,\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{M})&|\ \textit{for all}\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket,\ ((\rho\mathop{,}\mathit{x}\mapsto\mathit{W}),\mathit{M})\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\ \}\\
\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathop{\&}\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}&=&\{\ \mathbf{clo}(\rho,\langle\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle)&|\ (\rho,\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}})\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\ \textit{and}\ (\rho,\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\ \}\\
\\
\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}&=&\{\ (\rho,\mathit{M})&|\ \rho\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\ \textit{and}\ \mathit{T}\,\in\,\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{and}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}\ \}\\
\end{array}

We use this relation to define semantic typing for
environments, values, and computations.

Definition 2.3 (CBPV with Effects: Semantic Typing
11
11

11

effects/CBPV/semtyping.v:SemVWt,SemCWt
​
).

\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\ }l}\Gamma\vDash\rho&=&\lx@intercol\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\,\in\,\Gamma\ \textit{implies}\ \mathit{x}\mapsto\mathit{W}\,\in\,\rho\ \textit{and}\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}&=&\lx@intercol\Gamma\vDash\rho\ \textit{implies}\ \rho\vdash\mathit{V}\ \Downarrow\ \mathit{W}\ \textit{and}\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}&=&\lx@intercol\Gamma\vDash\rho\ \textit{implies}\ (\rho,\mathit{M})\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\end{array}

Using these definitions, we can prove semantic typing lemmas corresponding to
each of the syntactic typing rules shown in
Figure
1
. These proofs require our assumptions about the
monoidal structure of effects: that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}
is an identity element for the
associative combining operation.

With these lemmas, we show the fundamental lemma as a straightforward
induction.

Lemma 2.4 (Fundamental Lemma: effect soundness
12
12

12

effects/CBPV/soundness.v:fundamental_lemma
​
).

(1)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}
then
\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}
then
\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}
.

The effect soundness theorem (
2.1
) follows from the second
clause of this lemma, after instantiating
\Gamma
with the empty context and
\mathit{B}
with
\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}
.
Unfolding the definition of
\varnothing\vDash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}
gives us some
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\phi_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\phi_{2}}
such
that
\emptyset\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{T}\,\in\,\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}
.
Further unfolding definitions means that

\mathit{T}
must be
\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{W}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\phi_{2}}
must be
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}
, and thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}
.

2.4.
Type-and-effect Preserving Translations

Levy 2006
provides translations from call-by-value (CBV) and
call-by-name (CBN)
\lambda
-calculi to CBPV and shows that those translations
preserve types, denotational semantics, and (substitution-based) big-step
operational semantics. We show here that those
translations also preserve effects.

For the CBV translation, we start with a
\lambda
-calculus that has a
simple type-and-effect system, loosely based on

Lucassen and Gifford 1988
. However, as few CBN
languages directly include effects, for the CBN
translation we start with a simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus
that encapsulates effects
using a
graded monad
. Furthermore,
we show that we can use this same monad with the CBV translation because
effects are encapsulated.

2.4.1.
CBV Type-and-effect System

The simple CBV language with effect tracking
13
13

13

effect/CBV/typing.v:Wt
​
in this subsection features the
same
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}
term and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{Tick}}
effect as before, along with the usual forms of the
\lambda
-calculus.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\mathit{types}&\tau&::=&\texttt{unit}\ |\ \tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\ |\ \tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\otimes\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\ |\ \tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\\
\mathit{terms}&\mathit{e}&::=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}\ |\ \mathit{x}\ |\ \lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}\ |\ \mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\ |\ ()\ |\ \mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
&&|&(\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\ |\ \mathbf{let}\;(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}})=\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
&&|&\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{e}\ |\ \mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{e}\ |\ \mathbf{case}\;\mathit{e}\;\mathbf{of}\;\mathbf{inl}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rightarrow\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathbf{;}\mathbf{inr}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rightarrow\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\end{array}

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\tau

(STLC + effect typing)

\frac{\mathit{x}:\tau\,\in\,\Gamma}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{x}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}}\tau}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:lam-eff-var}{{{lam-eff-var}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}}\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:lam-eff-abs}{{{lam-eff-abs}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\quad\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}}\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:lam-eff-app}{{{lam-eff-app}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}():^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}}\mathbf{unit}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:lam-eff-unit}{{{lam-eff-unit}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathbf{unit}\quad\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\tau}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}\tau}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:lam-eff-sequence}{{{lam-eff-sequence}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\quad\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}(\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\otimes\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:lam-eff-pair}{{{lam-eff-pair}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\otimes\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\quad\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathop{,}\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\tau}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathbf{let}\;(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}})=\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}\tau}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:lam-eff-split}{{{lam-eff-split}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{e}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:lam-eff-inl}{{{lam-eff-inl}}}

Function types, written
\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, are annotated with
latent
effects
, which occur when the function is called. In the application rule
rule
lam-eff-app
, this latent effect is
combined with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
,
the effects that occur when evaluating the function

\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
to a
\lambda
expression, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, the effects that occur when
evaluating the argument to a value.

The CBV type
and term translations

\begin{array}[]{ll}\textit{Type translation}\\
\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;(\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\to\mathbf{F}\,\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}})\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{unit}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\texttt{unit}\\
\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\otimes\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\times\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\\
\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}+\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\\
&\\
\textit{Context translation}\\
\llbracket{\varnothing}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\varnothing\\
\llbracket{\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\\
&\\
\textit{Term translation}\\
\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}\\
\llbracket{\mathit{x}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{x}\\
\llbracket{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathbf{return}\,\{\lambda\mathit{x}.\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\}\\
\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{y}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{x}!\,\mathit{y}\\
\llbracket{()}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathbf{return}\,()\\
\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{x};\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\\
\llbracket{(\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}})}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{y}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathbf{return}\,(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{let}\;(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}})=\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathbf{let}\;(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}})=\mathit{x}\;\mathbf{in}\;\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathbf{return}\,(\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{x})\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathbf{return}\,(\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{x})\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{case}\;\mathit{e}\;\mathbf{of}\;\mathbf{inl}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rightarrow\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathbf{;}\mathbf{inr}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rightarrow\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathbf{case}\,\mathit{x}\,\mathbf{of}\,\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\to\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\mathsf{;}\,\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\to\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\\
\end{array}

This translation preserves types and effects from the source language.

Lemma 2.5 (CBV translation is type correct

​
).

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\mathit{e}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\tau

\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathbf{F}\,\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}
.

This result is easy to prove, reassuring us that our
effect system design is correct: we can use CBPV to encode the well-studied
type-and-effect systems developed over the past 40 years.

2.4.2.
Graded Monads

CBPV is designed to serve as a convenient translation
target for both CBV and CBN languages.
However, in CBN languages, effects are usually
16
16

16

Instead of graded monads, we could also consider a translation from call-by-name language that does not
encapsulate effects, such as the one defined by
McDermott and Mycroft 2018
.

tracked using parametric effect
monads, also known as
graded monads

(
Katsumata 2014
;
Smirnov 2008
;
Wadler and Thiemann 2003
;
Orchard and Petricek 2014
)
.
Therefore, here we translate a CBN language
with graded monads

\lambda
-calculus with unit, sums and products, together with
a graded monadic type

and
\mathbf{bind}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}

for the graded monad.

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{mon}}\mathit{e}:\tau

(STLC + graded monad)

Below, we extend Levy’s translation of the CBN
\lambda
-calculus
18
18

18

effects/CBN/translation:translateTerm
​
to include the graded
monad. The translation of the core language is as in prior work and all effects
are isolated to the monadic type, so we only show the monadic portion in the figure.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\textit{Type translation}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{unit}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{F}\,\mathbf{unit}\\
\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\to\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=(\mathbf{U}\,\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}})\to\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\\
\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathop{\&}\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\mathop{\&}\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\\
\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{F}\,(\mathbf{U}\,\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}+\mathbf{U}\,\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}})\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{F}\,(\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathbf{F}\,(\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}}\;\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}))\\
&\\
\textit{Context translation}\\
\llbracket{\varnothing}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\varnothing\\
\llbracket{\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}}\;\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\\
&\\
\textit{Term translation}\\
\llbracket{\mathit{x}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathit{x}!\\
\llbracket{\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\lambda\mathit{x}.\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\\
\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\,\{\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\}\\
\llbracket{()}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{return}\,()\\
\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{x};\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\\
\llbracket{\langle\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\langle\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}},\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\rangle\\
\llbracket{\mathit{e}.1}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}.1\\
\llbracket{\mathit{e}.2}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}.2\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{return}\,\mathbf{inl}\,\{\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{return}\,\mathbf{inr}\,\{\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{case}\;\mathit{e}\;\mathbf{of}\;\mathbf{inl}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rightarrow\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathbf{;}\mathbf{inr}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rightarrow\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathbf{case}\,\mathit{x}\,\mathbf{of}\,\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\to\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\mathsf{;}\,\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\to\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{return}\,\{\mathbf{return}\,\{\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\}\}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{bind}\,\mathit{x}=\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{return}\,\{\mathit{x}\leftarrow(\mathit{y}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{y}!)\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{z}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{z}!\}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{coerce}\,\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{return}\,\{\mathit{x}\leftarrow\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathit{x}!\}\\
\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{return}\,\{\mathit{x}\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathbf{return}\,\{\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{x}\}\}\\
\end{array}

This translation preserves types (with embedded effects) from the source
language. Note that, because the monadic type marks effectful code, the
translation produces CBPV computations that can be checked with the “pure”
effect
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}
.

Lemma 2.6 (CBN translation is type correct
19
19

19

effects/CBN/proofs.v:translation_correct
​
).

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{mon}}\mathit{e}:\tau
then

\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}}\;\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}
.

One difficulty of this translation is that the monadic type in the CBPV
adjunction is
U F
. This type is a value type, and the standard CBN
translation produces terms with computation types. Therefore. to use
U
F
as the monad in our CBN translation, we need to bracket it: on the
outside by
F
to form a computation type, and then on the inside by

U
to construct the value type that the monad expects. This bracketing
produces an awkward translation of the monadic operations with doubled
thunking. This awkwardness is due to the presence of the monad in the source
language; it is a separate structure from the ambient monad of the computation
language.

Because the graded monad isolates effects, we can also
evaluate the monadic language using a call-by-value semantics,
reusing the same translation we used for the CBV language with
effects. For the CBV translation, the monadic type is more accessible: the type translation produces value
types, so we don’t need the additional bracketing in the translations for
\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{e}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}
. The translations for
\mathbf{bind}
and
\mathbf{coerce}
remain unchanged.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\textit{Type translation}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathbf{F}\,\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\\
&\\
\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{ll}\textit{Term translation}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathbf{return}\,\{\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\}\\
\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathbf{return}\,\{\mathit{x}\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}\,\mathbf{in}\,\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{x}\}\\
&\\
\end{array}

Lemma 2.7 (Monadic translation type correctness
20
20

20

effects/CBV/proofs.v:translation_correct
​
).

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{mon}}\mathit{e}:\tau
then
\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\vdash_{\mathit{eff}}\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}}\;\mathbf{F}\,\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}
.

3.
CBPV and Coeffects (Version 1: General semantics)

Next, we construct a parallel extension of CBPV augmented with
coeffect

tracking. Appendix
B
lists the
typing rules,
21
21

21

general/typing.v:VWt,CWt
​
with coeffect annotations in
blue. Coeffect systems are designed for reasoning about how programs use their
inputs, so we annotate variables at their binding sites and in the context.

Coeffects annotations consist of
grades

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
taken from a

preordered semiring
. This structure provides an addition operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
,
an additive identity element
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
, a multiplication
operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, a multiplicative identity
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}
,
and a reflexive and transitive binary relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}
that respects
addition and multiplication. (The preorder does not have to be the one defined
by the addition operation.) The need for a semiring rather than a monoid
arises from the fact that any value may be bound to a variable
that may then be used multiple times, requiring a notion of coeffect multiplication.

Similarly to the previous section, our type system in this section is general
across coeffects and can be specialized via the choice of semiring and
preorder. For example, if we are only concerned with relevance analysis
(i.e. determining which of a functions inputs are relevant to computation)
then we might use a semiring with two elements:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
marks inputs that are
known to be unused and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
is for elements that may or may not be
needed. Or, in the case of information flow, then we might use a semiring
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
marks secret inputs and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
marks public information; only
the latter may influence the result of the computation.

We would also like to use coeffects to track resource usage. However, as we
discuss in detail below, this general semantics does not provide a satisfying
account of resource usage and requires further refinement in the next
section. Therefore, we first describe the general semantics in terms of the
resource usage coeffect, so that we can prepare for this discussion.

In the case of resource usage, grades bound the
uses
of variables, as
in bounded linear logic, and come from the natural number semiring with the
usual addition and multiplication operators. The additive and multiplicative
identity elements of this semiring mark
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
and at most
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
(affine)
use of a variable respectively, and the addition and multiplication semiring
operations calculate the total number of times each variable is used in the
program.

As in many systems for bounded linear logic,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
indicates that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
is
less precise
or
less restrictive

than
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
. When counting variable usage, this has the
opposite order from the usual one—we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}
because allowing at
most 3 uses is less restrictive than at most 2. With other coeffects, such as
security levels, this ordering has a more intuitive interpretation: a higher
grade corresponds to a higher security level, which is more restrictive than a
low security level.

Like the effect system with subeffecting,
this type system includes a rule for
subcoeffecting
: if
a judgment holds with some annotation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
on a variable
in the context, then it is also derivable with any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.
For example, we can weaken a judgment
that a computation makes zero (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
) uses of some variable to observe at most one
use (affine) or any other number. This corresponds to the usual weakening
lemma from typed
\lambda
-calculi.

Again, as in the effect section, including a preorder with the semiring allows for
imprecision,
needed when analyzing branching computations. For example, if one
branch requires
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
use of a variable
\mathit{x}
, but the other branch
requires
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
uses, the system will record that the program must have
the resources to use
\mathit{x}
at least once, because

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
, in a semiring where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
corresponds to affine usage.
This relation is dual to the preorder’s role in
the effect system—if one branch ticks once and the other does not tick,
then the system will record at most one tick.
In both cases, replacing the ordering with the
discrete preorder means that the type system must be precise and would
reject both of these examples.

The type system uses a
grade vector

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
, a comma-separated list of
grades, to represent the annotations for the variables in a typing context.
When combined with a typing context
\Gamma
, written
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma
,
the grade vector must have the same length as
\Gamma
.
We extend a combined grade vector and typing context simultaneously with the
notation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{A}
, equivalent to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}\mathop{,}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}})}\!\cdot\!(\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A})
.

The grade vector written
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{0}}
contains
only zeros and is used where its length can be inferred from context.
Grade vectors of the same length can be added together pointwise, written

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, and compared pointwise, written
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.
Grade vectors can also be pointwise scaled, written
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}
.

The basis of this system is rule
coeff-var
. When
introducing a variable
\mathit{x}
, the context must grade
\mathit{x}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
,
indicating that it is used once.
No other variables in the context should affect
the typing judgement, so they must have grade
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
.
Similarly, the unit value
()
can make no demands on the environment, so rule
coeff-unit
requires
that all variables in the typing context be graded
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
.

In rule
coeff-thunk
, rule
coeff-inl
,
rule
coeff-inr
, and rule
coeff-force
, there is a single subterm that
makes exactly the same demands on its environment as the term in the
conclusion, so we use the same grade vector in the conclusion and the premise.
For example, in a sum type,
\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{V}
makes the same demands as

\mathit{V}
.

In other rules, the term in the conclusion has multiple subterms, so we
combine the demands made by each. In rule
coeff-pair
,
the subterms both get evaluated and do not directly
interact, so we combine their grade vectors via simple pointwise addition.
Conversely, with negative products, the two subterms must use the same resources,
so we use the same grade
vector in each premise and the conclusion.
Intuitively, this is because we can only ever
project out one subterm from a computation pair (see rule
coeff-fst
and
rule
coeff-snd
), so the projected term will make all the same demands on
the environment as the pair.

In rule
coeff-abs
, we know from the premise that
\mathit{M}
will require a
grade of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
on
\mathit{x}
, so we store that grade as an annotation on

\mathit{x}
in the term syntax. For flexibility, we allow the annotation in the
type,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}
, to be a less precise approximation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
. (This
expressiveness is useful for the translation results in the next section.
Note that subcoeffecting is not sufficient as it cannot allow the annotation
on the
\lambda
to differ from the annotation on the function type.)
Both the premise and the
conclusion make the same demands on the variables in
\Gamma
, so
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}

is otherwise the same in both.

In some rules, we must
combine the grade vectors of subterms using both scaling and addition.
For example, in rule
coeff-app
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
denotes
the demands the operator
\mathit{M}
makes on the environment, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}

denotes the demands the argument
\mathit{V}
makes.

\mathit{M}
has type
\mathit{A}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\rightarrow\mathit{B}
, indicating that
when it is reduced to some terminal
\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}}.\mathit{M^{\prime}}
, then
\mathit{M^{\prime}}
will
require
\mathit{x}
to have a grade of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}
.
This means we must scale

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
before adding it to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
to calculate
the total demands that
\mathit{M}\,\mathit{V}
makes on its environment.

Rule
coeff-split
follows a similar pattern. In this rule, we
require a grade of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
on
\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in
\mathit{N}
, so we scale

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
, or the demands made by
\mathit{V}
, by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
. (Subcoeffecting
allows us to use the same grade for
\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
even though
the exact demands
\mathit{N}
makes on each may be different.)

In rule
coeff-case
, we additionally require that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}
.
We need to evaluate
\mathit{V}
to either
\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
or
\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
for some

\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
or
\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
in order for this branching to be well-defined. In
our resource usage example, we can interpret this as requiring
at least
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
copy of
\mathit{V}
in order to proceed.

In the effect system, we annotate the type
\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}
with the effect of
the suspended computation. In the coeffect system, we dually annotate the returner
type
\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathit{A}
. In our resource usage example, the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}

indicates that we require enough resources from the environment to
produce
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
copies of a value. For example,
\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}\;\mathit{V}
indicates
that we require the resources to create
3
copies of
\mathit{V}
.
Therefore, rule
coeff-ret
scales the demands needed to create

\mathit{V}
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
.

In rule
coeff-letin
,
\mathit{M}
has returner type
\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\mathit{A}
, and its
result value has been scaled by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
. However, the expression includes
another scaling annotation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, that allows duplication of the computation

\mathit{M}
itself. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
denotes the demands
\mathit{M}
makes on its environment,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
denotes the grade
\mathit{N}
requires
\mathit{x}
to have, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}

denotes the demands
\mathit{N}
makes from the rest of the environment, then we need

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
to type the entire term.

The scaling annotations in
\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathit{V}
and
\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{M}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{N}
increase
the expressiveness of the language and are required for the translation of a
CBV
\lambda
-calculus to CBPV described in
Section
4.1.2
. Because CBV is strict, when
translating an application, we must use a let binding to evaluate the
translated argument before applying the translated function to it.
However, the function may require a particular grade
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
on its argument,
so we must be able to scale this computation. Similarly, to translate the graded CBV
comonadic type, we need to be able to duplicate values.

The two subsumption rules
coeff-vsub
and
coeff-csub
allow for subcoeffecting.

3.1.
General Instrumented Operational Semantics and Coeffect Soundness

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}

(Computation rules)

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}.\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}}.\mathit{M})}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-abs}{{{eval-coeff-comp-abs}}}
