\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\!\cdot\!\rho^{\prime},\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}.\mathit{M^{\prime}})\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathit{W}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\!\cdot\!\rho^{\prime}\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W}\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M^{\prime}}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\,\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-app-abs}{{{eval-coeff-comp-app-abs}}}
}{},width=3in}\!\mpr@andcr\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}\Downarrow(\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathbf{case}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathit{V}\;\mathbf{of}\;(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\;\rightarrow\;\mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-split}{{{eval-coeff-comp-split}}}
}{},}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathit{W}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-return}{{{eval-coeff-comp-return}}}
}{},}\ \mpr@andcr\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathit{W}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}\mathit{W}\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{M}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-letin-ret}{{{eval-coeff-comp-letin-ret}}}
}{},}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\!\cdot\!\rho^{\prime},\{\mathit{M}\})\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\!\cdot\!\rho^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}!\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-force-thunk}{{{eval-coeff-comp-force-thunk}}}
}{},width=4in}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\!\cdot\!\rho^{\prime},\langle\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle)\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\!\cdot\!\rho^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}.1\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-fst}{{{eval-coeff-comp-fst}}}
}{},}\ \mpr@andcr\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\!\cdot\!\rho^{\prime},\langle\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle)\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\!\cdot\!\rho^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}.2\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-snd}{{{eval-coeff-comp-snd}}}
}{},}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}\Downarrow()\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V};\mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-sequence}{{{eval-coeff-comp-sequence}}}
}{},width=5in}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{W}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W}\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathbf{case}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathit{V}\;\mathbf{of}\;\mathbf{inl}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rightarrow\;\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\mathbf{inr}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rightarrow\;\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-case-inl}{{{eval-coeff-comp-case-inl}}}
}{},width=5in}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{W}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W}\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathbf{case}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathit{V}\;\mathbf{of}\;\mathbf{inl}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rightarrow\;\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}};\mathbf{inr}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rightarrow\;\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-case-inr}{{{eval-coeff-comp-case-inr}}}
}{},width=5in}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-coeff-comp-csub}{{{eval-coeff-comp-csub}}}
}{},width=5in}

Figure 3
.
Instrumented operational semantics (computations)
Rules for the instrumented operational semantics

Next, we develop an instrumented
operational semantics
22
22

22

general/semantics.v:EvalVal,EvalComp
​
, shown in Figure
2
and Figure
3
, that tracks coeffects using an
environment
\rho
, which maps variables to closed values, and a grade
vector
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
of equal length, which implicitly maps variables to their
coeffects. As before, we extend both a grade vector and corresponding
environment simultaneously with the notation

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W}
, equivalent to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}\mathop{,}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}})}\!\cdot\!(\rho\mathop{,}\mathit{x}\mapsto\mathit{W})
.

We also use
\mathit{W}
as a metavariable
for
closed
values and

\mathit{T}
as a metavariable for
closed terminal
computations. However,
closed terminals include coeffects here. They have the form

\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W}
,
\mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}.\mathit{M})
, or

\mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\langle\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle)
, where
\mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\mathit{M})
denotes the

closure
of
\mathit{M}
under
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho
. The grade vector in
the closure indicates the demands on the variables used by
\mathit{M}
.

Unlike our instrumented operational semantics for effects, which calculates
the exact effect of a computation, this semantics cannot track
coeffects with precision.
For example, suppose we have a term
\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}.\mathit{M}

where
\mathit{M}
is a computation that both branches on its argument and uses it in at exactly one branch, such as

\mathbf{case}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\;\mathit{x}\;\mathbf{of}\;\mathbf{inl}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rightarrow\;\mathbf{return}\,\mathit{x};\mathbf{inr}\;\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rightarrow\;\mathbf{return}\,\mathbf{inr}\,()
.
What should this step to? If provided with an argument of the form
\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{y}
,
it should step to
\mathbf{clo}(\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{y},\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}.\mathit{M})
.
If provided with an argument of the form
\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{y}
, it should step to

\mathbf{clo}(\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{y},\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}.\mathit{M})
. But, if this term is the entire program,
it is not clear what it should step to.
In general, depending on the argument, the body of a function

\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}.\mathit{M}
may require a different exact grade
on
\mathit{x}
; all we know from the typing judgement is that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
must be a bound on that usage.
We cannot write a precise rule for evaluating abstractions
to their closed terminal forms, because we do not have access to the argument yet
when doing that evaluation.

Therefore, as in the typing rules, the operational semantics also includes rules
for subcoeffecting, rules
eval-coeff-val-vsub
and
eval-coeff-comp-csub
. These rules say
that if we can step a term with grades given by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
attached to the environment,
then we can step it with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}
for any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}
, i.e., any less
precise accounting.

As in the semantics for CBPV without coeffects, we define “evaluation” of
values using the given environment (see
Figure
2
).
These rules mirror the typing rules: rule
eval-coeff-val-var
requires the
evaluating variable to have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
as its corresponding grade and all other variables
to have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
;
rule
eval-coeff-val-unit

requires that every variable be graded with 0;
rule
eval-coeff-val-thunk
simply includes the grade vector in the closure along
with the environment, and rule
eval-coeff-val-vpair

sums the grades needed to evaluate subterms to their
closures.

Figure
3
also shows
computations.
Rules
eval-coeff-comp-abs
,
eval-coeff-comp-force-thunk
,
eval-coeff-comp-cpair
,
eval-coeff-comp-fst
, and
eval-coeff-comp-snd
are largely the same as before,
just with the inclusion of grade vectors along with environments.
Rule
eval-coeff-comp-sequence
simply sums the vectors required to evaluate each
subterm.
The sum type elimination rules scale the demands made by the term being
eliminated by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
before adding them to the demands needed to evaluate
the rest of the computation, as in the typing rules. They also require
that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}
for the branching behavior to be well-defined, as
in the typing rules. Intuitively, in a resource counting context, if we
have 0 copies of a value, we should not be able to use it to determine
which branch to take.

In rule
eval-coeff-comp-return
, we scale the grade needed to evaluate the
subterm to its closure by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
. In the elimination
rules
eval-coeff-comp-app-abs
,
eval-coeff-comp-letin-ret
, and
eval-coeff-comp-split
,
if we are eliminating a value
\mathit{V}
and binding it to a variable

\mathit{x}
with a grade
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
for use in some computation
\mathit{M}
,
we must scale the grade vector needed to evaluate
\mathit{V}

by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
before adding it to the grade vector needed to continue with
\mathit{M}
, as
in the typing rules.

We prove a coeffect soundness theorem stating that if a term is well-typed
with some grade vector
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
, then given
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
and some
environment
\rho
that provides values of the correct type for all free
variables, it can evaluate to a terminal. Because both values and computations
make demands on their inputs, we state this property for both.
We formalize the requirement on
\rho
as
\Gamma\vDash\rho
in our logical
relation below, and this theorem follows immediately from the fundamental lemma.

Theorem 3.1 (Coeffect soundness
23
23

23

general/soundness.v:soundness
​
).

Let
\Gamma
be a context and
\rho
an environment mapping all variables
in the domain of
\Gamma
to closed values of the expected type, such
that
\Gamma\vDash\rho
. Then:

(1)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathit{W}
for some closed value
\mathit{W}
.

(2)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}:\mathit{B}
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}
for some closed terminal
computation
\mathit{T}
.

The proof of coeffect soundness is similar to the proof
of effect soundness, and requires a similar logical relation.

Definition 3.2 (CBPV with General Coeffects: Logical Relation
24
24

24

general/semtyping.v:LRV,LRC
​
).

\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\ }l}\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathbf{U}\,\mathit{B}\rrbracket&=&\{\ \mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\{\mathit{M}\})&|\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\mathit{M})\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket\ \}\\
\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathbf{unit}\rrbracket&=&\{\ ()\ \}\\
\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\times\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket&=&\{\ (\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{2}}}})&|\ \mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket\ \textit{and}\ \mathit{W_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\}\\
\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket&=&\{\ \mathbf{inl}\,\mathit{W}&|\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket\}\ \cup\{\ \mathbf{inr}\,\mathit{W}|\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\}\\
\\
\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathit{A}\rrbracket&=&\{\ \mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W}&|\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\ \}\\
\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathit{A}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\rightarrow\mathit{B}\rrbracket&=&\{\ \mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}.\mathit{M})&|\ \textit{for all}\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket,(({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W}),\mathit{M})\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket\ \}\\
\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathop{\&}\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket&=&\{\ \mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\langle\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rangle)&|\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}})\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket\ \textit{and}\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket\ \}\\
\\
\textit{Closures}\\
\mathcal{V}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket&=&\{\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\mathit{V})&|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathit{W}\ \textit{and}\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\ \}\\
\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket&=&\{\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\mathit{M})&|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\ \textit{and}\ \mathit{T}\,\in\,\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket\ \}\\
\\
\end{array}

Definition 3.3 (CBPV with General Coeffects: Semantic Typing
25
25

25

general/semtyping.v:SemVWt,SemCWt
​
).

\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\ }l}\Gamma\vDash\rho&=&\lx@intercol\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}\,\in\,\Gamma\ implies\ \textit{exists}\ \mathit{W},\ \mathit{x}\mapsto\mathit{W}\,\in\,\rho\ \textit{and}\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\hfil\lx@intercol\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}&=&\lx@intercol\textit{for all}\ \rho,\Gamma\vDash\rho\ implies\ \textit{exists}\ \mathit{W},\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathit{W}\ \textit{and}\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\hfil\lx@intercol\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}:\mathit{B}&=&\lx@intercol\textit{for all}\ \rho,\Gamma\vDash\rho\ implies\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\mathit{M})\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\end{array}

We can now state the fundamental lemma, which derives the
soundness theorem as a corollary.

Theorem 3.4 (Fundamental lemma: coeffect soundness
26
26

26

general/soundness.v:fundamental_lemma
​
).

For all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
,
\Gamma
, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}
,
and
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
,
\Gamma
, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}:\mathit{B}
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}:\mathit{B}
.

We can show
3.1
by unfolding the definitions of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}:\mathit{B}
, which
give us the desired evaluations.

3.2.
A Strange Semantics?

The operational semantics and soundness proof in this section
work for any instantiation of the coeffect semiring. However, this semantics has
strange implications for the resource usage coeffect. Here, the soundness
theorem should say that if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}
, then the evaluation
of
\mathit{M}
used its variables at most the number of times indicated by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
says that a variable
\mathit{x}
has grade
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
, then
there should never be a use of rule
eval-coeff-val-var
with the variable
\mathit{x}
.

But, on closer examination of the operational semantics, this is not exactly
what this soundness theorem implies. Consider the following example:

\mathit{x}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\mathbf{U}\,(\mathbf{F}\,\mathbf{unit})\vdash_{\mathit{coeff}}\mathit{z_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\mathit{x}!\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\;():\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\;\mathbf{unit}

\mathit{x}!
does not contribute to the final result,
and the resources used in its evaluation are accordingly multiplied by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
when
we calculate the grade for
\mathit{x}
in the context. However, our
semantics evaluates
\mathit{x}
once here using rule
eval-coeff-val-var
,
violating the principle we described above.

More generally, we encounter this issue with any rule in the operational semantics
that scales resources based on some annotation in the terms. For example, in
rule
eval-coeff-comp-app-abs
, the resources used by the evaluation of the
argument
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
are scaled by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
, the grade on the function
argument. The total resources of the application
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
must equal
this scaled vector plus

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
, the resources used to evaluate the
function –
i.e.
, we must have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}
. What if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
? The resources
needed to compute the argument are then not accounted for in

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
. This suggests that we should not evaluate the argument at all in
this case, so we need to adjust our operational semantics.

4.
CBPV and coeffects (Version 2: Resource Tracking)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}:\mathit{B}

(Modified typing rule)

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}:\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\mathit{A}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}}}\mathit{A}\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{N}:\mathit{B}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}})+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{M}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{N}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:lin-letin}{{{lin-letin}}}
}{},width=8in}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}

(New and modified computation rules)

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\!\cdot\!\rho^{\prime},\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}.\mathit{M^{\prime}})\quad({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{\prime}}\!\cdot\!\rho^{\prime})\mathop{,}\;(\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\mathop{\lightning})\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M^{\prime}}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}\,\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-lin-comp-app-abs-zero}{{{eval-lin-comp-app-abs-zero}}}
}{},width=5in}

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{0}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\;\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\mathop{\lightning}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-lin-comp-ret-zero}{{{eval-lin-comp-ret-zero}}}

}{},width=3in}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathit{W}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}}}\mathit{W}\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{M}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-lin-comp-letin-ret}{{{eval-lin-comp-letin-ret}}}
}{},width=8in}

Figure 4
.
Typing rules and instrumented operational semantics for resource tracking
Modified computation rules for the instrumented operational semantics

In this section, we discuss how, with a few additional axioms, we can modify
our instrumented operational semantics and type system to produce a better
model for resource tracking. Our goal is to ensure that we never evaluate
values and computations without including their resource usage in the final count. The
modifications that we discuss here are summarized in
Figure
4
. We
use the judgements
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}:\mathit{B}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}

to refer to the modified typing rules
27
27

27

resource/CBPV/typing.v:
​
of this section and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}
to refer to the modified operational semantics
28
28

28

resource/CBPV/semantics.v:
​
,
highlighting the connection between resource usage coeffects and bounded linear logic.

First, we axiomatize that the semiring is nontrivial. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
, resource
tracking via grades is meaningless, and our general semantics degenerates to
standard CBPV. Second, we require
that if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
. If either
subterm in a value pair requires nonzero resources, we should not be
able to evaluate the pair with no resources. Finally, for similar reasons, we
require that there be no nonzero zero divisors in the semiring,
i.e.
,
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}
.
Semirings that satisfy these additional constraints include natural numbers, with their usual or discrete orderings, or the
\{0,1,\omega\}
semiring that tracks whether inputs are unused, only used linearly, or with any usage. Note that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
is incomparable to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
in this semiring.

In this system, the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
grade denotes that the corresponding variable is
inaccessible, i.e., used
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
times, so anywhere we eliminate a value and bind it to an
inaccessible variable (or return a value with grade
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
), we require special
treatment.
Rules
eval-coeff-comp-app-abs
,
eval-coeff-comp-return
, and
eval-coeff-comp-split
all have this property,
so we modify these rules to require that the relevant grade
be nonzero.
We also add new rules that apply when the grade

is
zero. These rules, shown in
Figure
4
, discard the unused value
\mathit{V}
without evaluating it
and use a new, untyped, closed value
\mathop{\lightning}
in place of the result of evaluating
\mathit{V}
.
Because values are pure, discarding an unused value does not alter any effects of the program.

However, rule
eval-coeff-comp-letin-ret
requires special consideration. Unlike in the
rules above, which discard values, this rule discards a
computation
– but
because that computation could be effectful, this could change the semantics in unintended ways.
Following related work
(
Gavazzo 2018
;
Dal Lago and Gavazzo 2022
)
,
we reconcile this by adding a notion of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
,
which is equivalent to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
when
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
is nonzero and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
otherwise.
We continue to allow the syntax of the term itself to contain any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, but the rest of the typing rule
refers to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
instead.
(All other typing rules stay the same.)
The evaluation rule, rule
eval-lin-comp-letin-ret
, follows the same pattern (see Figure
4
).
Note that this modified evaluation rule introduces a new source of imprecision: we may
consume resources to evaluate code without ever using its result, making our final resource
accounting more of an overapproximation.

With these modifications, we update our logical relation with
a special case for zero resources below.
For brevity we show only the changes.

Definition 4.1 (CBPV with Resource Coeffects: Logical Relation
29
29

29

resource/CBPV/semtyping.v:LRV,LRC
​
).

\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\ }l}\textit{Closed graded values}\\
\mathcal{W}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket&=&\{\ \mathop{\lightning}\ \}\\
\mathcal{W}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket&=&\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\mbox{ when ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\neq 0$}\\
\\
\textit{Closed terminals}\\
\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathit{A}\rrbracket&=&\{\ \mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W}&|\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\ \}\\
\mathcal{T}\llbracket\mathit{A}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\rightarrow\mathit{B}\rrbracket&=&\{\ \mathbf{clo}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho,\lambda\mathit{x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}.\mathit{M})&|\ \mathit{forall}\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket,\\
&&&\qquad(({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{W}),\mathit{M})\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket\ \}\\
\end{array}

Furthermore, we update our semantic typing relation for environments to also
include a special case for zero; in this case the environment need not have a closed value for that variable.
(The remaining definitions do not change other than to use the
resource accounting operational semantics. In particular,
\mathcal{V}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
still requires
the resulting closed value to be in
\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket
. )

Definition 4.2 (CBPV with Resource Coeffects: Semantic Typing
30
30

30

resource/CBPV/semtyping.v:SemVWt,SemCWt
​
).

\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\ }l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}\cdot\Gamma\vDash\rho&=&\lx@intercol\mathit{x}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{A}\,\in\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\ \mathit{implies}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}\ \textit{or}\ (\mathit{x}\mapsto\mathit{W}\,\in\,\rho\ \textit{and}\ \mathit{W}\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket)\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\end{array}

With these updates, we again prove the fundamental theorem. As in the previous
section, if we unfold the definitions above, this theorem gives us exactly the
soundness theorem
31
31

31

resource/CBPV/soundness.v:soundness
​
we would like.

Theorem 4.3 (Fundamental lemma: resource soundness
32
32

32

resource/CBPV/soundness.v:fundamental_lemma
​
).

For all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
,
\Gamma
, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}\cdot\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}
,
and
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
,
\Gamma
, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}:\mathit{B}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}\cdot\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}:\mathit{B}
.

We can also use this theorem to reason about unused variables. For example,
suppose we type check some computation
\mathit{M}
in the context of an
inaccessible variable
x
. Instantiating the theorem above with this context
assures us that evaluation succeeds even when variables are mapped to

\mathop{\lightning}
in the environment.

Corollary 4.4 (Inaccessible variable example).

For all
\mathit{M}
and
\mathit{B}
, if
\mathit{x}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\mathit{A}\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}:\mathit{B}
, then
there exists some
\mathit{T}
, such that
\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\mathop{\lightning}\vdash_{\mathit{lin}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}
.

Because the operational semantics does not include any rules for evaluating

\mathop{\lightning}
, we can conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}
-marked variables are never used by
the operational semantics. Furthermore, there are no assumptions about the
structure of
\mathop{\lightning}
values, so we can discard them during computation.

4.1.
Translation Soundness

As with effects, we explore the translation of coeffect-aware CBN and CBV

\lambda
-calculi to CBPV. As in our CBPV extension with coeffects, the source
type systems are parameterized by a preordered semiring structure of coeffects
and combine the typing context with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
, a vector of coeffect
annotations that describe the demands on each variable.

}{},width=5in}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{cbvcoeff}}\mathit{e}:\tau}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{cbvcoeff}}\mathbf{box}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\ \mathit{e}:\square_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}}\;\tau}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:cbvcoeff-box}{{{cbvcoeff-box}}}
}{},}\;\mpr@andcr\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{cbvcoeff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\square_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\tau\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}}}}\tau\vdash_{\mathit{cbvcoeff}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\tau^{\prime}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{cbvcoeff}}\mathbf{unbox}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\tau^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:cbvcoeff-unbox}{{{cbvcoeff-unbox}}}
}{},width=4in}

Figure 6
.
CBV with coeffect tracking
The typing rules for CBV augmented with coeffect tracking

The type-and-coeffect system that we consider as the starting point of our CBN
translation is adapted from the simple type system of

Choudhury et al. 2021
and is similar to the system developed by

Abel and Bernardy 2020
. The differences between this source language and the
related work are minor. The design of our CBV language is inspired by

Dal Lago and Gavazzo 2022
. To make the comparison clear, we present
it as a standard CBV lambda calculus instead of fine-grained CBV. Other
changes to the language include the introduction of subcoeffecting, allowing
functions to take
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
copies of their argument instead of one (and
annotating applications with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
), and replacing
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
to force the evaluation of subterms. (We choose
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}

over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
to avoid requiring the existence
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
as an axiom of the semiring. The difference is minor.)

The rules for the CBN version of the system appear in
Figure
5
33
33

33

resource/CBN/typing.v:Wt
​
; the rules for the CBV version are in
Figure
6
34
34

34

resource/CBV/typing.v:Wt
​
. Most of the rules
parallel those of the corresponding terms in CBPV; for brevity, then, we show
only the rules for application, boxing and unboxing here. The two languages
differ in the application rule. In CBV, we annotate applications with the number
of times the function uses its argument. Because the argument will always be
evaluated once in CBV, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
is zero, we force it to be one.

These latter two terms introduce and
eliminate the modal type
\square_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\tau
. The introduction form requires
grade
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
on its argument. When we unbox the argument, the second
subterm has access to it with grade
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
. The
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
comes from
when the box was created, and the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
comes from the unboxing term, as in
let bindings in CBPV. In CBV, we use
\mathbf{letin}
in
both translations, so we include
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
in both rules in an
analogous way to its use in rule
coeff-letin
.
In CBN, we use
\mathbf{letin}
in the translation of
\mathbf{unbox}
but
not
\mathbf{box}
, so we can drop
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
from the typing rule for box.
This imprecision makes sense in the source languages for the same reason it makes
sense in CBPV: because we are combining effects and coeffects,
we sometimes need to evaluate subterms for their effects even if the results of those
subterms are never used.

4.1.1.
Call-by-name Translation

We first consider a
call-by-name translation to CBPV. For brevity, we show just the translation
of function and box types on the left below and the translation of applications and
the
\mathbf{box}
and
\mathbf{unbox}
terms on the right.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\rightarrow\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=(\mathbf{U}\,\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}})^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\rightarrow\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\\
\llbracket{\square_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;(\mathbf{U}\,\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}})\\
\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{ll}\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\,\{\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{box}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\ \mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\{\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{unbox}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\ \mathbf{in}\ \llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{n}}\\
\end{array}

In this translation, the coeffect on the
\lambda
-calculus function
type translates directly
to the coeffect on the CBPV function type. Furthermore,
the modal type
\square_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\tau
is a graded comonad, so it can be translated to
the comonad in CBPV, adding the grade to the returner type.

The CBN translation of
\lambda
terms is as usual. However, the translation of the
box introduction and elimination forms follows from the definition of the CBPV comonadic type.
To create a box, we return the thunked translation of the expression. To eliminate a box,
we use
\mathbf{letin}
to move the thunk to the environment.

4.1.2.
Call-by-value Translation

Next, we define a corresponding CBV translation to CBPV. For brevity, we again
show only the translation of function and graded modal types and of applications and the

\mathbf{box}
and
\mathbf{unbox}
terms.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\rightarrow\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathbf{U}\,(\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\rightarrow\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\;\llbracket{\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}})\\
\llbracket{\square_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathbf{U}\,(\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}})\\
\\
\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{y}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{x}!\,\mathit{y}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{box}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\ \mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\llbracket{\mathit{e}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\;\{\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\;\mathit{x}\}\\
\llbracket{\mathbf{unbox}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}&=\mathit{y}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{y}!\ \mathbf{in}\ \llbracket{\mathit{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\rrbracket_{\textsc{v}}\\
\end{array}

As above, we propagate the coeffect from the
\lambda
-calculus
function type directly to the CBPV function type. Similarly, we propagate
the grade in the modal type to the inner returner type and let binding in CBPV.

For applications, we use let bindings to access the translations of the function and
the argument. The argument is not thunked in translation, so it is strict, but the
function is thunked in translation, so we must force it before applying it.

\mathbf{box}
is also strict in CBV, so its translation first
evaluates its argument. The rest of the translation follows its type
definition. In CBPV, the computation
\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\mathit{M}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\;\mathit{x}
is
equivalent to
\mathit{M}
, but the computation
\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{M}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathit{x}

corresponds to duplicating
\mathit{M}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
times in a resource usage coeffect.
This propagation of the grade is exactly the feature that we need to translate
the
\mathbf{box}
term. Like the CBN translation of the modal type, in the CBV
translation, the comonadic type is difficult to access. In this translation,

\mathbf{box}
must include an extra thunk that is forced in the translation of
the
\mathbf{unbox}
term, giving us access to the comonadic type
F U
.
We must also use the annotation capability of

\mathbf{letin}
(twice) to mirror the annotation in the source language. The
correctness proofs for both the CBN
35
35

35

resource/CBN/proofs.v:translation_correct
​
and
CBV
36
36

36

resource/CBV/proofs.v:translation_correct
​
translations follow from the
corresponding proofs of the combined system (taking the trivial effect).

5.
Combined Effects and Coeffects

Next, we present a system that tracks both effects and coeffects, by
combining the effect system of Section
2

with the resource usage system of Section
4
, and adding one
new rule.

Definition 5.1 (Combined type system
37
37

37

full/CBPV/typing.v:VWt,CWt
​
).

The judgements
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{V}:\mathit{A}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{M}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\mathit{B}
refer to the CBPV type system with
effect annotations from Figure
1
and coeffect annotations
(resource tracking version) from Figure
4
.The full definition is available in Appendix
C.1
.

This type system is a straightforward
combination of the systems presented earlier.
For example, the typing rule
full-letin
combines rule
eff-letin

with rule
lin-letin
and includes both the grade vector

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and the effect
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
for the
computation.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\mathit{A}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}\mathit{A}\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{B}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}})+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:full-letin}{{{full-letin}}}
}{},width=8in}

Similarly, we augment our instrumented operational semantics to track both
effects and coeffects.

Definition 5.2 (Combined Resource Semantics
38

full/CBPV/semantics.v:EvalVal,EvalComp
​
).
39
39

39

The full definition is available in Appendix

The judgements
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{V}\Downarrow\mathit{W}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}
refer to the CBPV operational semantics
with effect annotations from
Figures
A
and
A
and coeffect
annotations from Figure

, with updates for resource tracking from
Figure

.

For example, the
\mathbf{letin}
evaluation rule computes the
instrumented grade vector and effect and requires that the computation

\mathit{M}
be evaluated at least once, as in rule
eval-lin-comp-letin-ret
.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathit{W}\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}\mathit{W}\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{M}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-full-comp-letin}{{{eval-full-comp-letin}}}
}{},width=8in}

We can use this operational semantics to show both effect and coeffect soundness
of the combined type system. However, before we do so, we make one more extension
to the language.

Skipping Unused Discardable Computations

In Section
4
, we developed several “zero” rules
for discarding unused
values
. But, unused
computations
could not be
discarded, because they may have effects. However, in this system, we can identify
unused, pure computations, and add a new syntactic form, written
\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}\mathit{M}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{N}
,
indicating that they can be discarded. The typing rule (below left) requires
that
\mathit{M}
be effect free and its result unused in
\mathit{N}
.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{M}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}}\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathit{A}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\mathop{,}\mathit{x}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\mathit{A}\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{N}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\mathit{B}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}\mathit{M}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{N}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:full-letin-zero}{{{full-letin-zero}}}
}{},width=5in}\mpr@andcr\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\mathop{,}\;\mathit{x}\mapsto^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\mathop{\lightning}\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{x}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}\mathit{M}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{N}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:eval-full-comp-letin-zero}{{{eval-full-comp-letin-zero}}}
}{},width=5in}

Furthermore, the operational semantics of this new expression form (above
right) does not evaluate
\mathit{M}
. Instead it uses the junk value
\mathop{\lightning}

for the result of this computation.

To see this rule in action, consider the CBV translation of an expression

y_{2}\ (y_{1}\ x)
, where
y_{2}
is a constant function and
y_{1}
is pure. In this
case, the type system can observe that
\mathit{x}
does not contribute to the
final result when the application of
y_{1}
to
x
is marked as discardable.

\mathit{x}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\mathit{A}\mathop{,}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}}\;(\mathit{A}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\rightarrow\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A})\mathop{,}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;(\mathit{A}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}\rightarrow\mathit{B})\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{z}\leftarrow^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}!\,\mathit{x}\ \mathbf{in}\ \mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}!\,\mathit{z}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\mathit{B}

Soundness Proof for Discardable Computations

We next show that discarding unused values and unused pure computations
does not change the evaluation behavior of computations. To do so,
we need the following properties that state that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon}
is
the minimum element of the effect preorder.

40
40

40

These properties hold for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{tick}}
effects, but we have not used them
before now.

Definition 5.3 (Min identity).

(1) For all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}

(2) For all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}\ \textit{implies}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon}}
.

To prove that discarding is sound, we establish a relation between our
combined resource semantics and one that does not discard terms.

Definition 5.4 (Combined nondiscarding semantics
41
41

41

full/CBPV/junk.v:G.EvalComp
​
).

The judgement

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho\vdash_{\mathit{gen}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathit{T}\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}
refers to the operational semantics
that is the combination of CBPV with effect annotations from Figure

A
and coeffect annotations from
Figure
3
, with the modified rule
full-letin
that
always evaluates computations. This semantics does not include rules that
discard values or computations and uses rule
eval-full-comp-letin
to evaluate
the new
\mathbf{letin}
expression.

Our simulation lemma states that for closed boolean-valued
computations, evaluating with either the nondiscarding
semantics (Definition
5.4
) or with the resource
semantics (Definition
5.2
) produces the same result and the same
effect.

Lemma 5.5 (Resource Simulation
42
42

42

full/CBPV/junk.v:resource_simulation
​
).

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset}\!\cdot\!\varnothing\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{M}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}\;(\mathbf{unit}+\mathbf{unit})
then
either

(1)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset}\!\cdot\!\emptyset\vdash_{\mathit{gen}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}(\mathbf{inl}\,())\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset}\!\cdot\!\emptyset\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}(\mathbf{inl}\,())\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
or

(2)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset}\!\cdot\!\emptyset\vdash_{\mathit{gen}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}(\mathbf{inr}\,())\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\emptyset}\!\cdot\!\emptyset\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{M}\Downarrow\mathbf{return}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}}(\mathbf{inr}\,())\mathop{\#}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
.

This simulation lemma is a corollary of a much more general result—the
fundamental lemma for a binary logical relation between computations that are
evaluated with the two different semantics.
This relation appears in
Appendix
C.4
and
in the Coq development
43
43

43

full/CBPV/junk.v:LRM,LRV,LRC,SemVWt,SemCWt
​
.

Using this relation, we define a binary version of the semantic typing
relation. Two environments
\rho_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\rho_{{\mathrm{2}}}
are related when the
closed values in the first environment are related to themselves, and, if the
usage is nonzero, the closed value in the second environment is related to the
first. The first condition ensures that we know something about closed values
in the first relation even when the corresponding value in the second relation
has been discarded in the resource semantics.

Definition 5.6 (Semantic double typing

44

44

full/CBPV/junk.v:SemVWt,SemCWt

).

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ }c@{\ }ll}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\cdot\Gamma\vDash\rho_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sim\rho_{{\mathrm{2}}}&=&\mathit{x}:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\mathit{A}\,\in\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\Gamma\ \mathit{implies}\ \mathit{x}\mapsto\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\in\,\rho_{{\mathrm{1}}}\ \textit{and}\ (\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}})\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\ \mathit{and}\\
&&({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}\ \textit{or}\ (\mathit{x}\mapsto\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\,\in\,\rho_{{\mathrm{2}}}\ \textit{and}\ (\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket))\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\cdot\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sim\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\;:\mathit{A}&=&\mathit{forall}\ \rho_{{\mathrm{1}}},\rho_{{\mathrm{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\cdot\Gamma\vDash\rho_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sim\rho_{{\mathrm{2}}}\\
&&\mathit{implies}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Downarrow\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\ \textit{and}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\!\cdot\!\rho_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{V_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Downarrow\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\\
&&\mathit{and}\ (\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}})\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\ \textit{and}\ (\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{W_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,\in\,\mathcal{W}\llbracket\mathit{A}\rrbracket\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\cdot\Gamma\vDash_{\mathit{full}}\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\approx\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\;:^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathit{B}&=&\mathit{forall}\ \rho_{{\mathrm{1}}},\rho_{{\mathrm{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}}\cdot\Gamma\vDash\rho_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sim\rho_{{\mathrm{2}}}\ \mathit{implies}\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}\cdot\rho_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{1}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}\cdot\rho_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{M_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\in\mathcal{M}\llbracket\mathit{B}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\\
\end{array}

The fundamental theorem shows that this binary relation is reflexive.

This fundamental lemma combines and generalizes prior results of this
paper. In particular, it shows the effect-and-coeffect soundness of the
combined type system with respect to both the nondiscarding and resource
accounting semantics--the effects and coeffects of the evaluation are bounded
by the type system. For clarity, we also separately show effect-and-coeffect
soundness of the combined type system
in the Coq development

CBN and CBV Translations

Finally, we have defined CBN and CBV with combined effects and coeffects
and have proved the soundness of translations to the combined CPBV type system.

6.
Related Work

Call-by-push-value (CBPV) was originally developed by

Levy 2003b
.
Forster et al. 2019
mechanized proofs of its
metatheoretic properties
and translation soundness and
inspired our mechanized proofs. Current applications of CBPV include
modeling compiler intermediate
languages
(
Rizkallah et al. 2018
;
New 2019
)
,
understanding the role that polarity plays in bidirectional
typing
(
Dunfield and Krishnaswami 2021
)
and
subtyping
(
Lakhani et al. 2022
)
, and incorporating effects into
dependent type
theories
(
Pédrot et al. 2019
;
Pédrot and Tabareau 2019
)
.

CBPV and Effects

Call-by-value languages with effect tracking go back to
FX
(
Lucassen and Gifford 1988
)
.
Wadler and Thiemann 2003
showed the connection
between graded monads and effects by translating the effect system of

Talpin and Jouvelot 1994
to a language that isolates effects using graded
monads. Our monadic effect language is inspired by this paper, generalized
following
Katsumata 2014
. In this paper, our translation is the
reverse of Wadler and Thiemann, mapping a language with graded monads to an
effect-style extension of CBPV. Like us,
Rajani et al. 2021
use a
logical relation to show the soundness of their monadic cost analysis.

Although CBPV has often been used to model the semantics of effects, its type
system has only rarely been extended with effect tracking. The type system
that we present in Section
2
is most similar to MAM
(multi-adjunctive metalanguage) from
Forster et al. 2017
,
which builds on
Kammar and Plotkin 2012
and
Kammar et al. 2013
. Forster et
al. use MAM to compare the relative expressiveness of effect handlers, monadic
reflection and delimited control. The differences between our system and MAM
are in the abstract structure of effects: MAM does not use a
preordered monoid to track effects. Instead, in each extension effects are
interpreted differently. For effect handlers, effects are a set of operations
specified by some effect signature; for monadic reflection, effects are monad
stacks; for delimited control, effects are a stack of computation types.

Wuttke 2021
defines a cost-annotated version of CBPV by
annotating the thunk type in CBPV with a bound
[a<I]
that limits the number
of times that thunks can be forced. This work includes both call-by-value
and call-by-name translations from cost-annotated PCF terms to cost-annotated
CBPV. For expressiveness, the system includes subtyping and indexed
types.

Some extensions of CBPV annotate effects on
\mathbf{F}\,\mathit{A}
instead of
\mathbf{U}\,\mathit{B}
. These
systems isolate effects so that they need not be tracked by the typing judgement.
Extended Call-by-Push-Value
(ECBPV)
(
McDermott and Mycroft 2019
)
adds call-by-need evaluation to CBPV and
layers an effect system to augment equational reasoning. This system uses an
operation
\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}\rangle B
to extend the effect annotation to other
computation types, combining effects in returner types and pushing effects to
the result type of functions and inside with-products.

Rioux and Zdancewic 2020
tracks divergence. In this system, the sequencing operation requires that the
annotation on the returner type be less than or equal to any annotation on the
result of the continuation.

Coeffects

Type systems that track coeffects were introduced by

Brunel et al. 2014
;
Ghica and Smith 2014
;
Petricek et al. 2014
and developed by

Orchard et al. 2019
;
Orchard and Eades III 2022
;
Abel and Bernardy 2020
. Early applications were
for bounded linearity; but these systems have also been used for
tracking information flow in differential privacy
(
Reed and Pierce 2010
)
, dynamic binding
(
Nanevski 2003
)

and have also been applied for resource usage in Haskell
(
Bernardy et al. 2017
)
and
irrelevance in dependently-typed
languages
(
Atkey 2018
;
Choudhury et al. 2021
;
Abel et al. 2023
)
.

Petricek et al. 2014
give a number of additional examples, including dataflow (the
number of past values needed in a stream processing language) and data
liveness (whether references to a variable are still needed).

As in our work, all prior semantics that “count” uses of variables are
imprecise and allow execution to waste resources.
Abel et al. 2023
and

Choudhury et al. 2021
use a heap-based operational semantics to show
coeffect soundness for a language with a small-step, call-by-name semantics,
but do not consider the interactions with effects.
Bianchini et al. 2023

proves resource soundness for a fine-grained call-by-value language using a
big-step semantics. Their language includes a nontermination effect through
recursive functions and recursive types. Their soundness proof is based on a
heap-based semantics, which must simultaneously evaluate
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}
copies of an
expression. In contrast, because our environment-based semantics can separate
the resource usage of a subexpression from the rest of the computation, our
semantics uses multiplication instead of multi-usage. For consistency with
effects, several rules of their type system require that the number of copies
of the produced value to be nonzero, similar to our use of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\ \|\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
.

Dal Lago and Gavazzo 2022
also explore the addition of effects and
coeffects to a fine-grained call-by-value language. They also force the

\mathbf{letin}
term to count the coeffects of the computation at least once,
through the use of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}
. (This rule is derived from

Gavazzo 2018
.) Unlike our work, Dal Lago and Gavazzo give a
denotational semantics based on a monadic evaluation function and do not track
resource usage. Their main result is a definition of a program relation in the
presence of effects and coeffects. Their approach is to refine a standard
logical relation with
relators
and
corelators
that capture the
interaction of effects and coeffects with the language semantics. This
approach is more general than ours, which is tied to a specific effect and
coeffect.

CBPV and Linearity

Our extension of CBPV with coeffect typing is
novel and inspired by the duality with effects. The most related systems are
those involving linearity in the context of low-level or compiler intermediate
languages.
Schöpp 2015
develops a low-level language,
similar to CBPV, that includes linear operations in its type system. The

enriched effect
calculus

(
Egger et al. 2009
;
Egger et al. 2012
)

extends a type theory for computational effects, with primitives from linear
logic.
Ahmed et al. 2007
augment a variant of typed assembly language with
linear types.
Jang et al. 2024
develop a natural
deduction formulation of adjoint logic (which is similar to CBPV) and use its
structure to combine linear, affine, strict and intuitionistic logics in a
uniform setting.

Interactions Between Effects and Coeffects

Several systems describe interactions between effects and coeffects.

Nanevski 2003
uses comonads to guard the usage of local
state (dynamic binding) and monads to guard the usage of global state. In each
case the type system tracks the set of locations can be safely read and
updated. In future work, we would like to extend this work with state effects
and local effect handlers so that we can track this interaction using
annotations on thunk and returner types, instead of encapsulated within monad
and comonadic structures.

Gaboardi et al. 2016
present a combined calculus featuring effects and
coeffects. Unlike this work, their lambda calculus isolates effects and
coeffects using graded monadic and comonadic modal types. A key feature of
their system are “graded distributive laws”, that permit interactions
between the monad and comonad. The exact interactions are mediated by operations
determined by the particular effects and coeffects being modeled. For example,
we could distribute a term of type
\square_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}\;(\mathbf{T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2_{\mathit{eff}}}}}\;\tau)

into a term of type
\mathbf{T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}6_{\mathit{eff}}}}}\;(\square_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3}}\;\tau)
. That is, it could turn
3 copies of a monad which ticks twice and returns a term of type
\tau

into a monad which ticks 6 times then returns 3 copies of the term.

In future work, we hope to add distributivity to this system. Unlike the
distributive property described above, in this context the distributive laws
need not change the structure of the computation. Instead, we would like it to
redistribute grades on types in the form
\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}}}\;\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\mathit{A}
or

\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}}\;\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\mathit{B}
. However, we have yet to determine what sorts of
rearrangement are sound in this context.

7.
Conclusion and Future work

In this paper we have annotated the ambient monad and comonad of CBPV to
statically track effects and coeffects. We have presented these extensions
separately to provide a gentle introduction, before developing a combined
calculus that tracks both simultaneously. We have identified semantic subtleties
in resource tracking and have developed an alternative semantics that better
describes our understanding of this coeffect. We have proven soundness for all
versions of our type system, identifying the required assumptions of the
effect and coeffect algebras. To make sure that our designs are expressive, we
have shown the standard translations from call-by-value and call-by-name
lambda calculi into call-by-push-value preserve tick and resource tracking
with our system. By exploring both effects and coeffects together, we were
also able to observe similarities between these dual notions, and, more
importantly, identify their differences.

However, this work is only the starting point for investigation in this space.
The natural next step is to go beyond a single effect (tick) and single
coeffect (resource usage) to develop a more general structure for extensions
of CBPV, perhaps based on algebraic effects
(
Plotkin and Pretnar 2008
)
or effect
signatures
(
Katsumata 2014
)
. This structure would allow us to verify that
our rules stay general in the presence of other effects, such as
nontermination and state, or other coeffects, such as information-flow
tracking and differential privacy.

We can also extend this work by adding language features that interact with
effect and coeffect tracking, such as polymorphism, indexed or dependent
types, and quantification over effects and coeffects. Subtyping would captures
the idea that the type
\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\mathit{B}
is a subtype of
\mathbf{U}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\mathit{B}
when

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{eff}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phi}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, and that the type
\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\;\mathit{A}
is a subtype of

\mathbf{F}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\;\mathit{A}
when
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathop{\leq_{\mathit{co}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
. Finally, we would like to explore the
practical concerns of this system in more depth, focusing on how users or
compilers might make effective use of the statically tracked information.

Acknowledgements.

Thanks to Dominic Orchard, Richard Eisenberg and Kevin Diggs for comments
and suggestions. Yiyun Liu assisted with the initial setup of our Coq
proofs, building on a prior Autosubst development of CBPV in
Coq
(
Forster et al. 2019
)
. This work was supported by the
Sponsor National Science Foundation
under grants
Grant #CCF-2006535, Grant #CNS-2244494, and
Grant #CCF-2327738.
